RE conclusions rest on SciERC alone (preliminary).",
    "newString": "    \\item \\textbf{Multi-metric evaluation revealing metric dissociation.}\n    Five signals from three families are evaluated on quality estimation ($\\rho$), binary discrimination (AUROC), and sample selection (best-of-$N$ F1); these metrics decouple (EM leads AUROC at 0.749 but ranks third in $\\rho$), so single-metric evaluation risks selecting the wrong signal.\n\n    \\item \\textbf{LP score compression and MBR utility-quality misalignment.}\n    Despite strong quality estimation ($\\rho$ up to 0.38, AUROC up to 0.75), no signal significantly improves selection despite 12--18~pp of oracle headroom: LP scores are compressed into effective ties (60\\% of instances), and MBR-style selection correlates with F1 across instances but not within them.\n    Where compression is mitigated, LP becomes significant ($+2.9$~pp, $p{=}0.01$), confirming score compression as the bottleneck.\n\n    \\item \\textbf{Task-complexity boundary conditions.}\n    Signal rankings shift with difficulty (EM leads on easy tasks, SJ on hard), and at the capability boundary (WNUT-17), conditional correlations collapse to non-significance while LP exhibits strong negative correlation ($\\rho{=}{-}0.410$).\n    NER findings span three datasets; RE conclusions rest on SciERC alone (preliminary).",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\\label{sec:introduction}\n\nSelf-consistency---sampling multiple outputs and aggregating them---has become a powerful strategy for improving language model reasoning.\nOn discrete-answer tasks such as arithmetic and commonsense QA, majority voting over chain-of-thought samples yields large gains: +18 percentage points on GSM8K (PaLM-540B; \\citealp{wang2023selfconsistency}).\nMore broadly, test-time compute scaling through repeated sampling and selection has emerged as a complementary axis to model scaling, offering substantial quality improvements at inference time \\cite{snell2025scaling, brown2024monkeys}.\nThese results rest on a shared assumption: that among $N$ stochastic samples, a selection mechanism can identify better outputs.\nFor tasks with a single correct answer, this assumption holds naturally---the majority vote is the selector.\nBut information extraction (IE) poses a structurally different challenge.\nThe output is not a single token or number; it is a \\textit{set} of entities or relations with internal dependencies, where two samples may extract overlapping but non-identical structures.\nThere is no obvious majority to vote on, partial agreement is the norm rather than the exception, and the space of valid outputs is combinatorially large.\nCan self-consistency still provide useful signal in this structured setting?\n\nPrior work on confidence estimation for LLM-based IE has conflated two distinct objectives: \\textit{quality estimation} (which inputs does the model handle reliably?) and \\textit{sample selection} (which of $N$ outputs is best?).\nThese require different signal properties: the former benefits from instance-level aggregation, while the latter demands per-sample discrimination.\nSelf-consistency methods for discrete answers \\cite{wang2023selfconsistency, chen2024usc} and weighted variants \\cite{taubenfeld2025cisc, kang2025selfcertainty} assume outputs can be grouped by equivalence---an assumption that breaks for entity sets with partial overlap.\nThe most relevant study, \\citet{nedellec2026consistency}, examines consistency--accuracy correlations for LLM-based IE, finding results that our quality-estimation analysis corroborates.\nOur work complements theirs in three directions: (i)~structural matching beyond surface-level agreement, (ii)~testing whether quality estimation translates to best-of-$N$ selection (it does not), and (iii)~a difficulty gradient revealing non-obvious findings---WNUT-17 conditional correlations collapse to $\\rho{=}0.038$ (n.s.) and LP exhibits strong \\textit{negative} correlation ($\\rho{=}{-}0.410$)---that would be invisible on a single dataset.\n\nStructured IE requires schema-compliant output, making constrained decoding \\cite{willard2023guided} the standard inference strategy---without it, a substantial fraction of outputs from general-purpose models are unparseable.\\footnote{Fine-tuned models may retain format compliance without grammar constraints: our free-form ablation achieves 99.95\\% valid JSON rate (\\S\\ref{sec:ablation}).}\nAll experiments use constrained decoding, the regime under which structured IE is practically deployed.\n\nOur central finding is the \\textbf{correlation-selection gap} (Figure~\\ref{fig:overview}): consistency-based signals achieve strong quality estimation ($\\rho$ up to 0.38, AUROC up to 0.75) but fail at sample selection despite 12--18~pp of oracle headroom.\nFour of five signals are instance-level by construction---this is definitional, not empirical.\nThe non-trivial questions are whether (a)~consensus-proximity selection, instantiating MBR decoding \\cite{eikema2022sampling, freitag2022high, muller2021understanding}, can recover per-sample discrimination, and (b)~whether log-probability, the only per-sample signal, can exploit the headroom.\nBoth fail: MBR utility correlates with F1 across instances but not within them (\\S\\ref{sec:discussion}); log-probability scores are severely compressed (60\\% of instances effectively tied), though where compression is mitigated, LP becomes a significant selector ($+2.9$~pp, $p{=}0.01$).\nA controlled free-form decoding ablation confirms that NER LP compression persists without constrained decoding; on RE, LP selection reverses direction (preliminary), revealing task-dependent interaction with decoding regime (\\S\\ref{sec:ablation}).\n\n\\begin{figure*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\textwidth]{fig_overview}\n\\caption{The correlation-selection gap. \\textbf{Left}: high inter-sample agreement correlates with high F1 (quality estimation). \\textbf{Right}: instance-level signals assign identical scores to all candidates, precluding sample selection.}\n\\label{fig:overview}\n\\end{figure*}\n\nWe systematically compare five confidence signals from three families---structural (entity voting confidence [VC], Soft Jaccard [SJ]), surface (exact match [EM], Fleiss' $\\kappa$ [FK]), and model-internal (log-probability [LP])---across NER and RE on SciERC \\cite{luan2018scierc}, CoNLL-2003 \\cite{tjongkimsang2003conll}, and WNUT-17 \\cite{derczynski2017wnut}, using Qwen3-8B and LLaMA-3.1-8B.\nOur contributions are:\n\\begin{enumerate}\n    \\item \\textbf{Multi-metric evaluation revealing metric dissociation.}\n    We compare five confidence signals from three families---structural, surface, and model-internal---across three distinct metrics: quality estimation ($\\rho$), binary discrimination (AUROC), and sample selection (best-of-$N$ F1).\n    These metrics decouple: EM leads AUROC (0.749) but ranks third in $\\rho$ (0.295), so single-metric evaluation risks selecting the wrong signal for a given use case.\n\n    \\item \\textbf{LP score compression and MBR utility-quality misalignment.}\n    All signals achieve strong quality estimation ($\\rho$ up to 0.38, AUROC up to 0.75) yet none significantly improves selection despite 12--18~pp of oracle headroom.\n    The failure of four instance-level signals is definitional---they assign a single score per input and cannot rank candidates by construction.\n    The empirical contribution is diagnosing why the two per-sample mechanisms that \\textit{could} bridge the gap both fail: log-probability scores are compressed into effective ties (60\\% of instances within $\\varepsilon{=}0.05$), and consensus-proximity selection (MBR decoding) correlates with quality across instances but not within them.\n    Where compression is mitigated, LP becomes a significant selector ($+2.9$~pp, $p{=}0.01$), confirming score compression as the primary bottleneck.\n    A controlled ablation confirms LP compression persists without constrained decoding on NER; on RE, LP selection reverses direction (preliminary), indicating task-dependent interaction with decoding regime.\n\n    \\item \\textbf{Task-complexity boundary conditions.}\n    Signal rankings shift with task difficulty: EM leads on easy tasks, SJ on hard tasks---consistent across two model families at 8B scale, though with model-dependent magnitude.\n    At the capability boundary (WNUT-17, 52\\% zero F1), conditional correlations collapse to non-significance and LP exhibits strong negative correlation ($\\rho{=}{-}0.410$)---phenomena invisible on a single dataset that extend \\citet{nedellec2026consistency}'s quality-estimation analysis.\n    NER findings span three datasets; RE conclusions rest on SciERC alone (preliminary).\n\\end{enumerate}\n\n",

          " Our contributions are:",
          " \\begin{enumerate}",
          "     \\item \\textbf{Multi-metric evaluation revealing metric dissociation.}",
          "-    We compare five confidence signals from three families---structural, surface, and model-internal---across three distinct metrics: quality estimation ($\\rho$), binary discrimination (AUROC), and sample selection (best-of-$N$ F1).",
          "-    These metrics decouple: EM leads AUROC (0.749) but ranks third in $\\rho$ (0.295), so single-metric evaluation risks selecting the wrong signal for a given use case.",
          "+    Five signals from three families are evaluated on quality estimation ($\\rho$), binary discrimination (AUROC), and sample selection (best-of-$N$ F1); these metrics decouple (EM leads AUROC at 0.749 but ranks third in $\\rho$), so single-metric evaluation risks selecting the wrong signal.",
          " ",
          "     \\item \\textbf{LP score compression and MBR utility-quality misalignment.}",
          "-    All signals achieve strong quality estimation ($\\rho$ up to 0.38, AUROC up to 0.75) yet none significantly improves selection despite 12--18~pp of oracle headroom.",
          "-    The failure of four instance-level signals is definitional---they assign a single score per input and cannot rank candidates by construction.",
          "-    The empirical contribution is diagnosing why the two per-sample mechanisms that \\textit{could} bridge the gap both fail: log-probability scores are compressed into effective ties (60\\% of instances within $\\varepsilon{=}0.05$), and consensus-proximity selection (MBR decoding) correlates with quality across instances but not within them.",
          "-    Where compression is mitigated, LP becomes a significant selector ($+2.9$~pp, $p{=}0.01$), confirming score compression as the primary bottleneck.",
          "-    A controlled ablation confirms LP compression persists without constrained decoding on NER; on RE, LP selection reverses direction (preliminary), indicating task-dependent interaction with decoding regime.",

          "+    Where compression is mitigated, LP becomes significant ($+2.9$~pp, $p{=}0.01$), confirming score compression as the bottleneck.",
          " ",
          "     \\item \\textbf{Task-complexity boundary conditions.}",
          "-    Signal rankings shift with task difficulty: EM leads on easy tasks, SJ on hard tasks---consistent across two model families at 8B scale, though with model-dependent magnitude.",

          "+    Signal rankings shift with difficulty (EM leads on easy tasks, SJ on hard), and at the capability boundary (WNUT-17), conditional correlations collapse to non-significance while LP exhibits strong negative correlation ($\\rho{=}{-}0.410$).",

  "old_string": "\\paragraph{Difficulty-dependent signal complementarity.}\nOn SciERC NER (complex schema, 6 types), structural signals lead: VC achieves $\\rho{=}0.379$ and SJ $\\rho{=}0.360$, both outperforming surface ($\\rho{\\leq}0.295$) and LP ($\\rho{=}0.205$).\nThis advantage persists on RE (VC $\\rho{=}0.350$; preliminary, single benchmark), though all signals weaken due to the 40\\% zero-F1 floor effect.\nVC and SJ show moderate instance-level correlation (NER: $\\rho{=}0.659$; RE: $\\rho{=}0.605$), indicating information overlap but not redundancy; their differential behavior across difficulty levels reflects the complementary granularity of entity-level frequency vs.\\ span-level set overlap.\nWhile the best $\\rho{=}0.38$ (VC) explains approximately 14\\% of F1 variance, the best AUROC reaches 0.749 (EM), indicating strong binary discriminative power for the quality/failure classification most relevant to practitioners in quality-aware routing.\\footnote{Semantic entropy \\cite{kuhn2023semantic} achieves comparable AUROC on discrete-answer QA; direct comparison is not meaningful due to different output spaces.}\nPairwise bootstrap CIs confirm the SJ advantage over LP ($\\Delta\\rho{=}{+}0.155$, CI excludes zero) but not over EM ($\\Delta\\rho{=}{+}0.065$, marginal under Bonferroni correction; Table~\\ref{tab:bootstrap_signal_ci}, Appendix~\\ref{app:bootstrap_signal}).",
  "new_string": "\\paragraph{Difficulty-dependent signal complementarity.}\nOn SciERC NER (6 types), structural signals lead: VC $\\rho{=}0.379$, SJ $\\rho{=}0.360$, both outperforming surface ($\\rho{\\leq}0.295$) and LP ($\\rho{=}0.205$); this advantage persists on RE (VC $\\rho{=}0.350$; preliminary).\nThe best AUROC reaches 0.749 (EM), indicating strong binary discriminative power; pairwise bootstrap CIs confirm the SJ--LP gap ($\\Delta\\rho{=}{+}0.155$, CI excludes zero) but the SJ--EM gap is marginal (Appendix~\\ref{app:bootstrap_signal})."
}

{
  "replace_all": false,
  "file_path": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_1/projects/struct_self_consist_ie/docs/paper/sections/experiments.tex",
  "old_string": "\\paragraph{Signal rankings shift with task difficulty.}\nOn CoNLL (greedy F1${\\approx}$0.91), EM leads ($\\rho{=}0.451$, AUROC${=}0.816$), surpassing SJ and VC.\nWhen most samples are near-correct, binary agreement captures remaining variance more efficiently than set-level similarity.\nThis crossover---EM${>}$SJ on easy tasks vs.\\ SJ${>}$EM on hard tasks---demonstrates difficulty-dependent complementarity rather than a uniform structural advantage, suggesting practitioners should match signal type to expected task difficulty.\nStability across the two model families (both 8B) is strong: SJ achieves $\\rho{=}0.436$ (Qwen) vs.\\ $0.431$ (LLaMA) on CoNLL ($\\Delta{=}0.005$, within 3-seed $\\sigma{\\approx}0.007$).\nOn WNUT-17 ($N{=}16$), where 52\\% of instances receive zero F1, full-set correlations are misleading: VC achieves $\\rho{=}0.724$ (AUROC${=}0.913$), but this is entirely driven by the zero/non-zero boundary (conditional $\\rho{=}0.038$, n.s.; Table~\\ref{tab:wnut}).\nLP exhibits strong \\textit{negative} correlation ($\\rho{=}{-}0.410$, AUROC${=}0.219$).\nWe hypothesize that this reversal reflects overconfidence on out-of-distribution inputs \\citep{xiong2024llm}: the model assigns high log-probabilities to fluent but incorrect entity patterns (e.g., common noun phrases that resemble but do not match rare WNUT entity types), while producing lower scores for genuinely uncertain outputs where it correctly hedges.\nOn the conditional set, all signals collapse toward weak or non-significant correlations ($\\rho{\\leq}0.35$), with SJ achieving only $\\rho{=}0.089$ ($p{=}0.020$) on the full set---suggesting a capability boundary beyond which consistency signals capture binary success/failure but not quality gradation.",
  "new_string": "\\paragraph{Signal rankings shift with task difficulty.}\nOn CoNLL (greedy F1${\\approx}$0.91), EM leads ($\\rho{=}0.451$, AUROC${=}0.816$); this crossover---EM${>}$SJ on easy tasks vs.\\ SJ${>}$EM on hard tasks---demonstrates difficulty-dependent complementarity, consistent across model families ($\\Delta{=}0.005$, within 3-seed $\\sigma{\\approx}0.007$).\nOn WNUT-17 ($N{=}16$, 52\\% zero F1), full-set correlations are misleading: VC achieves $\\rho{=}0.724$ but conditional $\\rho{=}0.038$ (n.s.; Table~\\ref{tab:wnut}), while LP exhibits strong \\textit{negative} correlation ($\\rho{=}{-}0.410$), likely reflecting overconfidence on OOD inputs \\citep{xiong2024llm}.\nOn the conditional set, all signals collapse toward weak or non-significant correlations, suggesting a capability boundary beyond which consistency signals capture binary success/failure but not quality gradation."
}

    "oldString": "\\paragraph{Signal rankings shift with task difficulty.}\nOn CoNLL (greedy F1${\\approx}$0.91), EM leads ($\\rho{=}0.451$, AUROC${=}0.816$), surpassing SJ and VC.\nWhen most samples are near-correct, binary agreement captures remaining variance more efficiently than set-level similarity.\nThis crossover---EM${>}$SJ on easy tasks vs.\\ SJ${>}$EM on hard tasks---demonstrates difficulty-dependent complementarity rather than a uniform structural advantage, suggesting practitioners should match signal type to expected task difficulty.\nStability across the two model families (both 8B) is strong: SJ achieves $\\rho{=}0.436$ (Qwen) vs.\\ $0.431$ (LLaMA) on CoNLL ($\\Delta{=}0.005$, within 3-seed $\\sigma{\\approx}0.007$).\nOn WNUT-17 ($N{=}16$), where 52\\% of instances receive zero F1, full-set correlations are misleading: VC achieves $\\rho{=}0.724$ (AUROC${=}0.913$), but this is entirely driven by the zero/non-zero boundary (conditional $\\rho{=}0.038$, n.s.; Table~\\ref{tab:wnut}).\nLP exhibits strong \\textit{negative} correlation ($\\rho{=}{-}0.410$, AUROC${=}0.219$).\nWe hypothesize that this reversal reflects overconfidence on out-of-distribution inputs \\citep{xiong2024llm}: the model assigns high log-probabilities to fluent but incorrect entity patterns (e.g., common noun phrases that resemble but do not match rare WNUT entity types), while producing lower scores for genuinely uncertain outputs where it correctly hedges.\nOn the conditional set, all signals collapse toward weak or non-significant correlations ($\\rho{\\leq}0.35$), with SJ achieving only $\\rho{=}0.089$ ($p{=}0.020$) on the full set---suggesting a capability boundary beyond which consistency signals capture binary success/failure but not quality gradation.",
    "newString": "\\paragraph{Signal rankings shift with task difficulty.}\nOn CoNLL (greedy F1${\\approx}$0.91), EM leads ($\\rho{=}0.451$, AUROC${=}0.816$); this crossover---EM${>}$SJ on easy tasks vs.\\ SJ${>}$EM on hard tasks---demonstrates difficulty-dependent complementarity, consistent across model families ($\\Delta{=}0.005$, within 3-seed $\\sigma{\\approx}0.007$).\nOn WNUT-17 ($N{=}16$, 52\\% zero F1), full-set correlations are misleading: VC achieves $\\rho{=}0.724$ but conditional $\\rho{=}0.038$ (n.s.; Table~\\ref{tab:wnut}), while LP exhibits strong \\textit{negative} correlation ($\\rho{=}{-}0.410$), likely reflecting overconfidence on OOD inputs \\citep{xiong2024llm}.\nOn the conditional set, all signals collapse toward weak or non-significant correlations, suggesting a capability boundary beyond which consistency signals capture binary success/failure but not quality gradation.",
    "originalFile": null,
    "structuredPatch": [
      {

        "lines": [

          "-This crossover---EM${>}$SJ on easy tasks vs.\\ SJ${>}$EM on hard tasks---demonstrates difficulty-dependent complementarity rather than a uniform structural advantage, suggesting practitioners should match signal type to expected task difficulty.",
          "-Stability across the two model families (both 8B) is strong: SJ achieves $\\rho{=}0.436$ (Qwen) vs.\\ $0.431$ (LLaMA) on CoNLL ($\\Delta{=}0.005$, within 3-seed $\\sigma{\\approx}0.007$).",
          "-On WNUT-17 ($N{=}16$), where 52\\% of instances receive zero F1, full-set correlations are misleading: VC achieves $\\rho{=}0.724$ (AUROC${=}0.913$), but this is entirely driven by the zero/non-zero boundary (conditional $\\rho{=}0.038$, n.s.; Table~\\ref{tab:wnut}).",
          "-LP exhibits strong \\textit{negative} correlation ($\\rho{=}{-}0.410$, AUROC${=}0.219$).",
          "-We hypothesize that this reversal reflects overconfidence on out-of-distribution inputs \\citep{xiong2024llm}: the model assigns high log-probabilities to fluent but incorrect entity patterns (e.g., common noun phrases that resemble but do not match rare WNUT entity types), while producing lower scores for genuinely uncertain outputs where it correctly hedges.",
          "-On the conditional set, all signals collapse toward weak or non-significant correlations ($\\rho{\\leq}0.35$), with SJ achieving only $\\rho{=}0.089$ ($p{=}0.020$) on the full set---suggesting a capability boundary beyond which consistency signals capture binary success/failure but not quality gradation.",
          "+On CoNLL (greedy F1${\\approx}$0.91), EM leads ($\\rho{=}0.451$, AUROC${=}0.816$); this crossover---EM${>}$SJ on easy tasks vs.\\ SJ${>}$EM on hard tasks---demonstrates difficulty-dependent complementarity, consistent across model families ($\\Delta{=}0.005$, within 3-seed $\\sigma{\\approx}0.007$).",

          "+On the conditional set, all signals collapse toward weak or non-significant correlations, suggesting a capability boundary beyond which consistency signals capture binary success/failure but not quality gradation.",

In a two-column format, line numbers from pdftotext don't directly map to physical position, so I should check whether Discussion and Conclusion both fit on the same page or if Conclusion spans into the right column.

Looking at the line counts, Conclusion occupies roughly lines 73-100, about 28 lines. With a two-column layout where pdftotext linearizes the text, page 9's 187 total lines likely means each column has around 93-94 lines. So Conclusion starting at line 73 places it near the end of the left column.

When I compressed the Discussion section—removing about 23 lines of main text and replacing it with 3 lines, plus condensing another 7 lines to 2—I reduced the overall content significantly. I need to account for these removals when calculating how much space the main content now occupies.

Adding in the seed variability compression, difficulty paragraph reduction, signal rankings condensation, and Introduction contributions shortening, I'm looking at around 51 lines removed total. In a two-column EMNLP format with roughly 100-110 lines per page, that's approximately half a page of savings.

But that seems conservative given how much Discussion content I moved. Let me recount the Discussion section more carefully—those paragraphs from lines 27-49 were substantial.

Actually, I think the discrepancy is that I'm counting LaTeX source lines when I should be thinking about rendered lines. A single source line often wraps to multiple rendered lines in a column. The MBR paragraph alone is probably 12 rendered lines, the free-form section closer to 30, and the task-specific section another 30. That's roughly 72 rendered column lines removed, which translates to about 1.4 columns or roughly 0.7 pages—still not quite as much space as I'd hoped to recover.

Let me just look directly at where the Conclusion ends in the PDF rather than trying to estimate. If it starts around line 73 on page 9 and the section itself is fairly compact at about 10 LaTeX lines, it probably wraps to around line 100, which is where Limitations begins. <details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01BM3rqiXBksrhucmqo7FqKD`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Discussion}\label{sec:discussion}
2	
3	\paragraph{Practical recommendations.}
4	For per-instance quality ranking, VC provides the strongest rank correlation ($\rho{=}0.38$), though EM leads binary discrimination (AUROC${=}0.749$); under conditional filtering, EM leads on both NER and RE ($\rho{=}0.360$, $0.417$; RE: preliminary, single benchmark).
5	Signal rankings depend on task difficulty---EM dominates easy tasks (CoNLL), VC and SJ dominate hard tasks (SciERC)---directionally consistent across two model families at 8B scale, though with model-dependent magnitude.
6	At higher sample sizes ($N{=}16$), SJ converges to comparable NER performance with VC ($\Delta{=}0.002$) with lower cross-seed variance ($\sigma{=}0.010$ vs.\ $0.026$); on RE, only VC consistently outperforms LP (preliminary).
7	When model capability is insufficient (WNUT-17), all signals converge toward non-significant conditional correlations (Table~\ref{tab:wnut}).
8	
9	\paragraph{Why the gap exists: two layers.}
10	\textit{Layer 1 (definitional):} Four of five signals are instance-level aggregates that assign a single score per input; they cannot rank candidates by construction.
11	Consensus-proximity selection (\S\ref{sec:evaluation}) does not recover per-sample discrimination (Table~\ref{tab:selection}).
12	\textit{Layer 2 (score compression):} Log-probability, the only per-sample signal, also fails to exploit the 12--18~pp oracle headroom.
13	LP scores are severely compressed: the median within-instance LP range is only 0.041 nats, below the $\varepsilon{=}0.05$ threshold at which 60\% of instances are effectively tied, and 75\% of pairwise comparisons fall within $|\Delta|{<}0.026$ (Appendix~\ref{app:logprob_ties}).
14	In the 40\% of instances where compression is mitigated (LP range ${\geq}0.05$, $n{=}210$), LP \textit{significantly} outperforms greedy ($+2.9$~pp, $p{=}0.01$; within-instance $\rho$: 0.08$\to$0.39).
15	This diagnostic confirms that LP carries useful per-sample quality information; score compression is the primary mechanistic bottleneck.
16	A controlled NER ablation (\S\ref{sec:ablation}) confirms that LP compression persists without constrained decoding; on RE, the interaction is task-dependent.
17	Diversity-stratified analysis confirms the gap persists even among high-diversity instances (Appendix~\ref{app:diversity}); RE miscalibration (ECE 0.109--0.339, preliminary; Appendix~\ref{app:calibration}) may compound this.
18	
19	\paragraph{The gap widens with $N$.}
20	Doubling from $N{=}8$ to $N{=}16$ expands oracle headroom by $+4.4$~pp, yet LP improves by only $+0.4$~pp and VC deteriorates ($\Delta$: $-2.5{\to}{-}3.1$~pp); on RE at $N{=}16$, all consistency signals fall below greedy while LP is the sole positive signal ($\Delta{=}{+}0.7$~pp, $p{=}0.058$; Table~\ref{tab:re_n16_selection}).
21	The gap is not a small-$N$ artifact; detailed $N$-scaling analysis in Appendix~\ref{app:n_scaling}.
22	
23	\paragraph{Ablation: free-form decoding and MBR.}\label{sec:ablation}
24	Our selection strategies instantiate MBR decoding \cite{eikema2022sampling, freitag2022high}; all variants fail because utility functions correlate with F1 across instances but not within them (Appendix~\ref{app:mbr_discussion}).
25	A controlled free-form ablation on SciERC confirms LP compression persists without constrained decoding (within-instance std ratio $0.97{\times}$, tied fraction $59.7\%{\to}57.7\%$), while RE LP selection reverses direction ($-0.68{\to}+1.69$~pp; preliminary, single seed; Table~\ref{tab:freeform_ablation}, Appendix~\ref{app:freeform_ablation}).
26	
27	\paragraph{Methodological implications.}
28	The decoupling of quality estimation ($\rho$), binary discrimination (AUROC), and sample selection (best-of-$N$ F1) means evaluating on a single metric risks selecting the wrong signal for the intended use case.
29	Effective selection likely requires per-sample signals such as trained verifiers \cite{cobbe2021verifiers, venktesh2025trust}; $N$-scaling analysis (Appendix~\ref{app:n_scaling}) shows $N{=}8$ captures most quality estimation benefit.
30	Cross-dataset evaluation confirms that structural signals are robust to domain shift when the training schema subsumes the target (VC $\rho{=}0.469$ OOD vs.\ $0.379$ in-domain); the reverse direction fails due to catastrophic model failure, not signal failure (Appendix~\ref{app:ood}).
31	Our findings are established under constrained decoding at 8B scale; a single-dataset NER ablation suggests LP compression persists without constrained decoding, but RE shows task-dependent interaction with decoding regime.
32	Whether these patterns generalize across datasets, model scales, and decoding strategies warrants further investigation.
33	
```

under conditional filtering, EM leads on both NER and RE ($\\rho{=}0.360$, $0.417$; RE: preliminary, single benchmark).\nSignal rankings depend on task difficulty---EM dominates easy tasks (CoNLL), VC and SJ dominate hard tasks (SciERC)---directionally consistent across two model families at 8B scale, though with model-dependent magnitude.\nAt higher sample sizes ($N{=}16$), SJ converges to comparable NER performance with VC ($\\Delta{=}0.002$) with lower cross-seed variance ($\\sigma{=}0.010$ vs.\\ $0.026$); on RE, only VC consistently outperforms LP (preliminary).\nWhen model capability is insufficient (WNUT-17), all signals converge toward non-significant conditional correlations (Table~\\ref{tab:wnut}).\n\n\\paragraph{Why the gap exists: two layers.}\n\\textit{Layer 1 (definitional):} Four of five signals are instance-level aggregates that assign a single score per input; they cannot rank candidates by construction.\nConsensus-proximity selection (\\S\\ref{sec:evaluation}) does not recover per-sample discrimination (Table~\\ref{tab:selection}).\n\\textit{Layer 2 (score compression):} Log-probability, the only per-sample signal, also fails to exploit the 12--18~pp oracle headroom.\nLP scores are severely compressed: the median within-instance LP range is only 0.041 nats, below the $\\varepsilon{=}0.05$ threshold at which 60\\% of instances are effectively tied, and 75\\% of pairwise comparisons fall within $|\\Delta|{<}0.026$ (Appendix~\\ref{app:logprob_ties}).\nIn the 40\\% of instances where compression is mitigated (LP range ${\\geq}0.05$, $n{=}210$), LP \\textit{significantly} outperforms greedy ($+2.9$~pp, $p{=}0.01$; within-instance $\\rho$: 0.08$\\to$0.39).\nThis diagnostic confirms that LP carries useful per-sample quality information; score compression is the primary mechanistic bottleneck.\nA controlled NER ablation (\\S\\ref{sec:ablation}) confirms that LP compression persists without constrained decoding; on RE, the interaction is task-dependent.\nDiversity-stratified analysis confirms the gap persists even among high-diversity instances (Appendix~\\ref{app:diversity}); RE miscalibration (ECE 0.109--0.339, preliminary; Appendix~\\ref{app:calibration}) may compound this.\n\n\\paragraph{The gap widens with $N$.}\nDoubling from $N{=}8$ to $N{=}16$ expands oracle headroom by $+4.4$~pp, yet LP improves by only $+0.4$~pp and VC deteriorates ($\\Delta$: $-2.5{\\to}{-}3.1$~pp); on RE at $N{=}16$, all consistency signals fall below greedy while LP is the sole positive signal ($\\Delta{=}{+}0.7$~pp, $p{=}0.058$; Table~\\ref{tab:re_n16_selection}).\nThe gap is not a small-$N$ artifact; detailed $N$-scaling analysis in Appendix~\\ref{app:n_scaling}.\n\n\\paragraph{Ablation: free-form decoding and MBR.}\\label{sec:ablation}\nOur selection strategies instantiate MBR decoding \\cite{eikema2022sampling, freitag2022high}; all variants fail because utility functions correlate with F1 across instances but not within them (Appendix~\\ref{app:mbr_discussion}).\nA controlled free-form ablation on SciERC confirms LP compression persists without constrained decoding (within-instance std ratio $0.97{\\times}$, tied fraction $59.7\\%{\\to}57.7\\%$), while RE LP selection reverses direction ($-0.68{\\to}+1.69$~pp; preliminary, single seed; Table~\\ref{tab:freeform_ablation}, Appendix~\\ref{app:freeform_ablation}).\n\n\\paragraph{Methodological implications.}\nThe decoupling of quality estimation ($\\rho$), binary discrimination (AUROC), and sample selection (best-of-$N$ F1) means evaluating on a single metric risks selecting the wrong signal for the intended use case.\nEffective selection likely requires per-sample signals such as trained verifiers \\cite{cobbe2021verifiers, venktesh2025trust}; $N$-scaling analysis (Appendix~\\ref{app:n_scaling}) shows $N{=}8$ captures most quality estimation benefit.\nCross-dataset evaluation confirms that structural signals are robust to domain shift when the training schema subsumes the target (VC $\\rho{=}0.469$ OOD vs.\\ $0.379$ in-domain); the reverse direction fails due to catastrophic model failure, not signal failure (Appendix~\\ref{app:ood}).\nOur findings are established under constrained decoding at 8B scale; a single-dataset NER ablation suggests LP compression persists without constrained decoding, but RE shows task-dependent interaction with decoding regime.\nWhether these patterns generalize across datasets, model scales, and decoding strategies warrants further investigation.\n",
      "numLines": 33,
      "startLine": 1,
      "totalLines": 33
    }
  },

}

  "old_string": "\\paragraph{Methodological implications.}\nThe decoupling of quality estimation ($\\rho$), binary discrimination (AUROC), and sample selection (best-of-$N$ F1) means evaluating on a single metric risks selecting the wrong signal for the intended use case.\nEffective selection likely requires per-sample signals such as trained verifiers \\cite{cobbe2021verifiers, venktesh2025trust}; $N$-scaling analysis (Appendix~\\ref{app:n_scaling}) shows $N{=}8$ captures most quality estimation benefit.\nCross-dataset evaluation confirms that structural signals are robust to domain shift when the training schema subsumes the target (VC $\\rho{=}0.469$ OOD vs.\\ $0.379$ in-domain); the reverse direction fails due to catastrophic model failure, not signal failure (Appendix~\\ref{app:ood}).\nOur findings are established under constrained decoding at 8B scale; a single-dataset NER ablation suggests LP compression persists without constrained decoding, but RE shows task-dependent interaction with decoding regime.\nWhether these patterns generalize across datasets, model scales, and decoding strategies warrants further investigation.",
  "new_string": "\\paragraph{Methodological implications.}\nThe decoupling of $\\rho$, AUROC, and selection F1 means single-metric evaluation risks selecting the wrong signal.\nEffective selection likely requires per-sample signals such as trained verifiers \\cite{cobbe2021verifiers, venktesh2025trust}; $N{=}8$ captures most quality estimation benefit (Appendix~\\ref{app:n_scaling}).\nStructural signals are robust to domain shift when the training schema subsumes the target (VC $\\rho{=}0.469$ OOD vs.\\ $0.379$ in-domain; Appendix~\\ref{app:ood})."

    "oldString": "\\paragraph{Methodological implications.}\nThe decoupling of quality estimation ($\\rho$), binary discrimination (AUROC), and sample selection (best-of-$N$ F1) means evaluating on a single metric risks selecting the wrong signal for the intended use case.\nEffective selection likely requires per-sample signals such as trained verifiers \\cite{cobbe2021verifiers, venktesh2025trust}; $N$-scaling analysis (Appendix~\\ref{app:n_scaling}) shows $N{=}8$ captures most quality estimation benefit.\nCross-dataset evaluation confirms that structural signals are robust to domain shift when the training schema subsumes the target (VC $\\rho{=}0.469$ OOD vs.\\ $0.379$ in-domain); the reverse direction fails due to catastrophic model failure, not signal failure (Appendix~\\ref{app:ood}).\nOur findings are established under constrained decoding at 8B scale; a single-dataset NER ablation suggests LP compression persists without constrained decoding, but RE shows task-dependent interaction with decoding regime.\nWhether these patterns generalize across datasets, model scales, and decoding strategies warrants further investigation.",
    "newString": "\\paragraph{Methodological implications.}\nThe decoupling of $\\rho$, AUROC, and selection F1 means single-metric evaluation risks selecting the wrong signal.\nEffective selection likely requires per-sample signals such as trained verifiers \\cite{cobbe2021verifiers, venktesh2025trust}; $N{=}8$ captures most quality estimation benefit (Appendix~\\ref{app:n_scaling}).\nStructural signals are robust to domain shift when the training schema subsumes the target (VC $\\rho{=}0.469$ OOD vs.\\ $0.379$ in-domain; Appendix~\\ref{app:ood}).",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Discussion}\\label{sec:discussion}\n\n\\paragraph{Practical recommendations.}\nFor per-instance quality ranking, VC provides the strongest rank correlation ($\\rho{=}0.38$), though EM leads binary discrimination (AUROC${=}0.749$); under conditional filtering, EM leads on both NER and RE ($\\rho{=}0.360$, $0.417$; RE: preliminary, single benchmark).\nSignal rankings depend on task difficulty---EM dominates easy tasks (CoNLL), VC and SJ dominate hard tasks (SciERC)---directionally consistent across two model families at 8B scale, though with model-dependent magnitude.\nAt higher sample sizes ($N{=}16$), SJ converges to comparable NER performance with VC ($\\Delta{=}0.002$) with lower cross-seed variance ($\\sigma{=}0.010$ vs.\\ $0.026$); on RE, only VC consistently outperforms LP (preliminary).\nWhen model capability is insufficient (WNUT-17), all signals converge toward non-significant conditional correlations (Table~\\ref{tab:wnut}).\n\n\\paragraph{Why the gap exists: two layers.}\n\\textit{Layer 1 (definitional):} Four of five signals are instance-level aggregates that assign a single score per input; they cannot rank candidates by construction.\nConsensus-proximity selection (\\S\\ref{sec:evaluation}) does not recover per-sample discrimination (Table~\\ref{tab:selection}).\n\\textit{Layer 2 (score compression):} Log-probability, the only per-sample signal, also fails to exploit the 12--18~pp oracle headroom.\nLP scores are severely compressed: 60\\% of instances are effectively tied at $\\varepsilon{=}0.05$ (Appendix~\\ref{app:logprob_ties}).\nIn the 40\\% where compression is mitigated ($n{=}210$), LP \\textit{significantly} outperforms greedy ($+2.9$~pp, $p{=}0.01$), confirming score compression as the primary bottleneck; a free-form ablation (\\S\\ref{sec:ablation}) shows this persists without constrained decoding.\nThe gap holds even among high-diversity instances (Appendix~\\ref{app:diversity}).\n\n\\paragraph{The gap widens with $N$.}\nDoubling from $N{=}8$ to $N{=}16$ expands oracle headroom by $+4.4$~pp, yet LP improves by only $+0.4$~pp and VC deteriorates ($\\Delta$: $-2.5{\\to}{-}3.1$~pp); on RE at $N{=}16$, all consistency signals fall below greedy while LP is the sole positive signal ($\\Delta{=}{+}0.7$~pp, $p{=}0.058$; Table~\\ref{tab:re_n16_selection}).\nThe gap is not a small-$N$ artifact; detailed $N$-scaling analysis in Appendix~\\ref{app:n_scaling}.\n\n\\paragraph{Ablation: free-form decoding and MBR.}\\label{sec:ablation}\nOur selection strategies instantiate MBR decoding \\cite{eikema2022sampling, freitag2022high}; all variants fail because utility functions correlate with F1 across instances but not within them (Appendix~\\ref{app:mbr_discussion}).\nA controlled free-form ablation on SciERC confirms LP compression persists without constrained decoding (within-instance std ratio $0.97{\\times}$, tied fraction $59.7\\%{\\to}57.7\\%$), while RE LP selection reverses direction ($-0.68{\\to}+1.69$~pp; preliminary, single seed; Table~\\ref{tab:freeform_ablation}, Appendix~\\ref{app:freeform_ablation}).\n\n\\paragraph{Methodological implications.}\nThe decoupling of quality estimation ($\\rho$), binary discrimination (AUROC), and sample selection (best-of-$N$ F1) means evaluating on a single metric risks selecting the wrong signal for the intended use case.\nEffective selection likely requires per-sample signals such as trained verifiers \\cite{cobbe2021verifiers, venktesh2025trust}; $N$-scaling analysis (Appendix~\\ref{app:n_scaling}) shows $N{=}8$ captures most quality estimation benefit.\nCross-dataset evaluation confirms that structural signals are robust to domain shift when the training schema subsumes the target (VC $\\rho{=}0.469$ OOD vs.\\ $0.379$ in-domain); the reverse direction fails due to catastrophic model failure, not signal failure (Appendix~\\ref{app:ood}).\nOur findings are established under constrained decoding at 8B scale; a single-dataset NER ablation suggests LP compression persists without constrained decoding, but RE shows task-dependent interaction with decoding regime.\nWhether these patterns generalize across datasets, model scales, and decoding strategies warrants further investigation.\n",

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_017cciauoEznPHpGVxKxKcns`</summary>

```text
85	\small
86	\caption{Best-of-$N$ selection F1 on SciERC ($N{=}8$, $T{=}1.0$). F1 is macro (instance-averaged), the natural metric for per-instance selection. NER: 3-seed mean${\pm}\sigma$ ($n{=}551$) with Fisher combined $p$ vs.\ greedy; RE: single seed (seed${=}42$, preliminary). Best non-oracle signal in \textbf{bold}. VC uses majority-threshold voting ($>{N/2}$) for selection, distinct from continuous QE (Eq.~\ref{eq:voting}). $^\dagger$Greedy baseline: a fixed sample from the $T{=}1.0$ candidate pool (not $T{=}0$ deterministic decoding), serving as the no-selection reference within the sampled distribution.}
87	\label{tab:selection}
88	\begin{tabular}{lccc}
89	\toprule
90	Method & NER F1 & $p$ & RE F1 \\
91	\midrule
92	Oracle & $0.745 \pm 0.004$ & --- & 0.585 \\
93	\midrule
94	LP           & $\mathbf{0.620} \pm 0.003$ & .54 & 0.395 \\
95	SJ           & $0.616 \pm 0.008$ & .75 & 0.386 \\
96	EM           & $0.609 \pm 0.005$ & ${>}.99$ & 0.372 \\
97	VC           & $0.595 \pm 0.007$ & ${>}.99$ & 0.359 \\
98	\midrule
99	Greedy$^\dagger$ & $0.620 \pm 0.002$ & --- & \textbf{0.404} \\
100	Random           & $0.582 \pm 0.005$ & --- & 0.354 \\
101	\bottomrule
102	\end{tabular}
103	\end{table}
104	
105	Despite strong quality estimation, confidence signals yield limited gains when used for best-of-$N$ selection (Table~\ref{tab:selection}, Figure~\ref{fig:selection_gap}).
106	Four of five signals are instance-level aggregates that assign a single score per input regardless of which sample is evaluated; they cannot rank candidates by construction.
107	The non-trivial question is whether log-probability---the only per-sample signal---can exploit the oracle headroom.
108	We anchor this analysis on SciERC, where output diversity is substantial: only 11--18\% of instances produce identical outputs across all $N{=}8$ samples, with 39--43\% unique outputs per instance (mean 3.5 distinct outputs), per-sample F1 standard deviation of 0.11--0.14, and mean pairwise SJ of 0.703.
109	
110	\paragraph{No significant improvement over greedy.}
111	We evaluate significance via 3-seed instance-level paired bootstrap resampling ($B{=}10{,}000$, Fisher combination; $n{=}551$).
112	On NER, the oracle (0.745, 3-seed mean) reveals 12.5~pp of headroom above greedy (0.620).
113	LP achieves the highest mean selection F1 (0.620), indistinguishable from greedy ($\Delta{=}{+}0.0$~pp, Fisher $p{=}0.54$).
114	On RE, the best signal (LP, 0.395) falls below greedy (0.404, $-0.9$\,pp, $p{=}0.80$; single seed), while 18.1~pp of oracle headroom remains; cross-seed analysis confirms RE LP failures are partially systematic (Appendix~\ref{app:lp_failure_stability}).
115	TOST equivalence testing confirms most signals are indistinguishable from greedy at ${\pm}2$--$3$~pp on NER; on RE, EM and VC fail equivalence even at ${\pm}5$~pp (Table~\ref{tab:tost}, Appendix~\ref{app:diversity}).
116	VC ($\rho{=}0.379$, the best quality estimator) selects---via majority-threshold voting, distinct from the continuous QE score---to only 0.595 on NER (3-seed mean), \textit{significantly below} greedy ($\Delta{=}{-}2.5$~pp, Fisher $p{<}0.001$).
117	The gap extends to CoNLL at $N{=}8$ (ceiling effect, 57--88\% identical outputs) and WNUT-17 (capability boundary; Table~\ref{tab:cross_selection}).
118	
119	\paragraph{Seed variability.}
120	Individual seeds exhibit notable variance: seed~456 produces uniformly negative deltas across all five signals, while even the most favorable seed (seed~42, LP $\Delta{=}{+}0.6$~pp) falls short of significance ($p{=}0.19$).
121	Single-seed evaluations may overstate or understate signal utility by several percentage points, motivating our multi-seed design.
122	
123	\paragraph{Quality estimation $\neq$ output selection.}
124	Instance-level confidence signals predict \textit{which inputs} the model handles reliably, not \textit{which output} among $N$ candidates is best.
125	When per-sample variance is low relative to instance-level difficulty, even perfect quality estimation cannot discriminate among near-identical candidates.
126	This gap has direct consequences for test-time scaling in IE \cite{brown2024monkeys, snell2025scaling}: closing it likely requires per-sample signals such as trained verifiers \cite{cobbe2021verifiers}.
127	
128	%% ============================================================
129	\subsection{Robustness}
130	\label{sec:robustness_main}
131	
132	Consistency-based signals show robust performance across $T{\in}\{0.7, 1.0\}$ (SJ NER $\Delta\rho{=}0.028$, bootstrap CI includes zero) and sublinear gains from $N{=}8$ to $N{=}16$, with structural signals maintaining their advantage over LP across all NER settings ($\Delta\rho{=}{+}0.135$ to ${+}0.175$; Table~\ref{tab:robustness}, Appendix~\ref{app:robustness}).
133	On RE, only VC consistently outperforms LP ($\Delta\rho{=}{+}0.012$, preliminary).
134	NER signals are reasonably calibrated (ECE 0.069--0.096; Appendix~\ref{app:calibration}), while RE miscalibration (ECE 0.109--0.339) and compound errors impose a ceiling at $\rho{\approx}0.28$ for $N{=}16$.
135	
```

        "newLines": 7,
        "lines": [
          " Despite strong quality estimation, confidence signals yield limited gains when used for best-of-$N$ selection (Table~\\ref{tab:selection}, Figure~\\ref{fig:selection_gap}).",
          " Four of five signals are instance-level aggregates that assign a single score per input regardless of which sample is evaluated; they cannot rank candidates by construction.",
          " The non-trivial question is whether log-probability---the only per-sample signal---can exploit the oracle headroom.",
          "-We anchor this analysis on SciERC, where output diversity is substantial: only 11--18\\% of instances produce identical outputs across all $N{=}8$ samples, with 39--43\\% unique outputs per instance (mean 3.5 distinct outputs), per-sample F1 standard deviation of 0.11--0.14, and mean pairwise SJ of 0.703.",
          "+We anchor this analysis on SciERC, where output diversity is substantial (39--43\\% unique outputs per instance, F1 std 0.11--0.14; Appendix~\\ref{app:diversity}).",
          " ",
          " \\paragraph{No significant improvement over greedy.}",
          " We evaluate significance via 3-seed instance-level paired bootstrap resampling ($B{=}10{,}000$, Fisher combination; $n{=}551$)."
        ]
      }

```text
1	\section{Introduction}\label{sec:introduction}
2	
3	Self-consistency---sampling multiple outputs and aggregating them---has become a powerful strategy for improving language model reasoning.
4	On discrete-answer tasks such as arithmetic and commonsense QA, majority voting over chain-of-thought samples yields large gains: +18 percentage points on GSM8K (PaLM-540B; \citealp{wang2023selfconsistency}).
5	More broadly, test-time compute scaling through repeated sampling and selection has emerged as a complementary axis to model scaling, offering substantial quality improvements at inference time \cite{snell2025scaling, brown2024monkeys}.
6	These results rest on a shared assumption: that among $N$ stochastic samples, a selection mechanism can identify better outputs.
7	For tasks with a single correct answer, this assumption holds naturally---the majority vote is the selector.
8	But information extraction (IE) poses a structurally different challenge.
9	The output is not a single token or number; it is a \textit{set} of entities or relations with internal dependencies, where two samples may extract overlapping but non-identical structures.
10	There is no obvious majority to vote on, partial agreement is the norm rather than the exception, and the space of valid outputs is combinatorially large.
11	Can self-consistency still provide useful signal in this structured setting?
12	
13	Prior work on confidence estimation for LLM-based IE has conflated two distinct objectives: \textit{quality estimation} (which inputs does the model handle reliably?) and \textit{sample selection} (which of $N$ outputs is best?).
14	These require different signal properties: the former benefits from instance-level aggregation, while the latter demands per-sample discrimination.
15	Self-consistency methods for discrete answers \cite{wang2023selfconsistency, chen2024usc} and weighted variants \cite{taubenfeld2025cisc, kang2025selfcertainty} assume outputs can be grouped by equivalence---an assumption that breaks for entity sets with partial overlap.
16	The most relevant study, \citet{nedellec2026consistency}, examines consistency--accuracy correlations for LLM-based IE, finding results that our quality-estimation analysis corroborates.
17	Our work complements theirs in three directions: (i)~structural matching beyond surface-level agreement, (ii)~testing whether quality estimation translates to best-of-$N$ selection (it does not), and (iii)~a difficulty gradient revealing non-obvious findings---WNUT-17 conditional correlations collapse to $\rho{=}0.038$ (n.s.) and LP exhibits strong \textit{negative} correlation ($\rho{=}{-}0.410$)---that would be invisible on a single dataset.
18	
19	Structured IE requires schema-compliant output, making constrained decoding \cite{willard2023guided} the standard inference strategy---without it, a substantial fraction of outputs from general-purpose models are unparseable.\footnote{Fine-tuned models may retain format compliance without grammar constraints: our free-form ablation achieves 99.95\% valid JSON rate (\S\ref{sec:ablation}).}
20	All experiments use constrained decoding, the regime under which structured IE is practically deployed.
21	
22	Our central finding is the \textbf{correlation-selection gap} (Figure~\ref{fig:overview}): consistency-based signals achieve strong quality estimation ($\rho$ up to 0.38, AUROC up to 0.75) but fail at sample selection despite 12--18~pp of oracle headroom.
23	Four of five signals are instance-level by construction---this is definitional, not empirical.
24	The non-trivial questions are whether (a)~consensus-proximity selection, instantiating MBR decoding \cite{eikema2022sampling, freitag2022high, muller2021understanding}, can recover per-sample discrimination, and (b)~whether log-probability, the only per-sample signal, can exploit the headroom.
25	Both fail: MBR utility correlates with F1 across instances but not within them (\S\ref{sec:discussion}); log-probability scores are severely compressed (60\% of instances effectively tied), though where compression is mitigated, LP becomes a significant selector ($+2.9$~pp, $p{=}0.01$).
26	A controlled free-form decoding ablation confirms that NER LP compression persists without constrained decoding; on RE, LP selection reverses direction (preliminary), revealing task-dependent interaction with decoding regime (\S\ref{sec:ablation}).
27	
28	\begin{figure*}[t]
29	\centering
30	\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{fig_overview}
31	\caption{The correlation-selection gap. \textbf{Left}: high inter-sample agreement correlates with high F1 (quality estimation). \textbf{Right}: instance-level signals assign identical scores to all candidates, precluding sample selection.}
32	\label{fig:overview}
33	\end{figure*}
34	
35	We systematically compare five confidence signals from three families---structural (entity voting confidence [VC], Soft Jaccard [SJ]), surface (exact match [EM], Fleiss' $\kappa$ [FK]), and model-internal (log-probability [LP])---across NER and RE on SciERC \cite{luan2018scierc}, CoNLL-2003 \cite{tjongkimsang2003conll}, and WNUT-17 \cite{derczynski2017wnut}, using Qwen3-8B and LLaMA-3.1-8B.
36	Our contributions are:
37	\begin{enumerate}
38	    \item \textbf{Multi-metric evaluation revealing metric dissociation.}
39	    Five signals from three families are evaluated on quality estimation ($\rho$), binary discrimination (AUROC), and sample selection (best-of-$N$ F1); these metrics decouple (EM leads AUROC at 0.749 but ranks third in $\rho$), so single-metric evaluation risks selecting the wrong signal.
40	
41	    \item \textbf{LP score compression and MBR utility-quality misalignment.}
42	    Despite strong quality estimation ($\rho$ up to 0.38, AUROC up to 0.75), no signal significantly improves selection despite 12--18~pp of oracle headroom: LP scores are compressed into effective ties (60\% of instances), and MBR-style selection correlates with F1 across instances but not within them.
43	    Where compression is mitigated, LP becomes significant ($+2.9$~pp, $p{=}0.01$), confirming score compression as the bottleneck.
44	
45	    \item \textbf{Task-complexity boundary conditions.}
46	    Signal rankings shift with difficulty (EM leads on easy tasks, SJ on hard), and at the capability boundary (WNUT-17), conditional correlations collapse to non-significance while LP exhibits strong negative correlation ($\rho{=}{-}0.410$).
47	    NER findings span three datasets; RE conclusions rest on SciERC alone (preliminary).
48	\end{enumerate}
49	
50	
```

</details>

    "oldString": "Our work complements theirs in three directions: (i)~structural matching beyond surface-level agreement, (ii)~testing whether quality estimation translates to best-of-$N$ selection (it does not), and (iii)~a difficulty gradient revealing non-obvious findings---WNUT-17 conditional correlations collapse to $\\rho{=}0.038$ (n.s.) and LP exhibits strong \\textit{negative} correlation ($\\rho{=}{-}0.410$)---that would be invisible on a single dataset.",
    "newString": "Our work complements theirs in three directions: (i)~structural matching beyond surface-level agreement, (ii)~testing whether quality estimation translates to best-of-$N$ selection (it does not), and (iii)~a difficulty gradient revealing non-obvious findings invisible on a single dataset.",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\\label{sec:introduction}\n\nSelf-consistency---sampling multiple outputs and aggregating them---has become a powerful strategy for improving language model reasoning.\nOn discrete-answer tasks such as arithmetic and commonsense QA, majority voting over chain-of-thought samples yields large gains: +18 percentage points on GSM8K (PaLM-540B; \\citealp{wang2023selfconsistency}).\nMore broadly, test-time compute scaling through repeated sampling and selection has emerged as a complementary axis to model scaling, offering substantial quality improvements at inference time \\cite{snell2025scaling, brown2024monkeys}.\nThese results rest on a shared assumption: that among $N$ stochastic samples, a selection mechanism can identify better outputs.\nFor tasks with a single correct answer, this assumption holds naturally---the majority vote is the selector.\nBut information extraction (IE) poses a structurally different challenge.\nThe output is not a single token or number; it is a \\textit{set} of entities or relations with internal dependencies, where two samples may extract overlapping but non-identical structures.\nThere is no obvious majority to vote on, partial agreement is the norm rather than the exception, and the space of valid outputs is combinatorially large.\nCan self-consistency still provide useful signal in this structured setting?\n\nPrior work on confidence estimation for LLM-based IE has conflated two distinct objectives: \\textit{quality estimation} (which inputs does the model handle reliably?) and \\textit{sample selection} (which of $N$ outputs is best?).\nThese require different signal properties: the former benefits from instance-level aggregation, while the latter demands per-sample discrimination.\nSelf-consistency methods for discrete answers \\cite{wang2023selfconsistency, chen2024usc} and weighted variants \\cite{taubenfeld2025cisc, kang2025selfcertainty} assume outputs can be grouped by equivalence---an assumption that breaks for entity sets with partial overlap.\nThe most relevant study, \\citet{nedellec2026consistency}, examines consistency--accuracy correlations for LLM-based IE, finding results that our quality-estimation analysis corroborates.\nOur work complements theirs in three directions: (i)~structural matching beyond surface-level agreement, (ii)~testing whether quality estimation translates to best-of-$N$ selection (it does not), and (iii)~a difficulty gradient revealing non-obvious findings---WNUT-17 conditional correlations collapse to $\\rho{=}0.038$ (n.s.) and LP exhibits strong \\textit{negative} correlation ($\\rho{=}{-}0.410$)---that would be invisible on a single dataset.\n\nStructured IE requires schema-compliant output, making constrained decoding \\cite{willard2023guided} the standard inference strategy---without it, a substantial fraction of outputs from general-purpose models are unparseable.\\footnote{Fine-tuned models may retain format compliance without grammar constraints: our free-form ablation achieves 99.95\\% valid JSON rate (\\S\\ref{sec:ablation}).}\nAll experiments use constrained decoding, the regime under which structured IE is practically deployed.\n\nOur central finding is the \\textbf{correlation-selection gap} (Figure~\\ref{fig:overview}): consistency-based signals achieve strong quality estimation ($\\rho$ up to 0.38, AUROC up to 0.75) but fail at sample selection despite 12--18~pp of oracle headroom.\nFour of five signals are instance-level by construction---this is definitional, not empirical.\nBoth per-sample mechanisms that could bridge the gap fail: MBR utility correlates with F1 across instances but not within them (\\S\\ref{sec:discussion}), and log-probability scores are compressed into effective ties (60\\% of instances), though where compression is mitigated, LP becomes significant ($+2.9$~pp, $p{=}0.01$; \\S\\ref{sec:ablation}).\n\n\\begin{figure*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\textwidth]{fig_overview}\n\\caption{The correlation-selection gap. \\textbf{Left}: high inter-sample agreement correlates with high F1 (quality estimation). \\textbf{Right}: instance-level signals assign identical scores to all candidates, precluding sample selection.}\n\\label{fig:overview}\n\\end{figure*}\n\nWe systematically compare five confidence signals from three families---structural (entity voting confidence [VC], Soft Jaccard [SJ]), surface (exact match [EM], Fleiss' $\\kappa$ [FK]), and model-internal (log-probability [LP])---across NER and RE on SciERC \\cite{luan2018scierc}, CoNLL-2003 \\cite{tjongkimsang2003conll}, and WNUT-17 \\cite{derczynski2017wnut}, using Qwen3-8B and LLaMA-3.1-8B.\nOur contributions are:\n\\begin{enumerate}\n    \\item \\textbf{Multi-metric evaluation revealing metric dissociation.}\n    Five signals from three families are evaluated on quality estimation ($\\rho$), binary discrimination (AUROC), and sample selection (best-of-$N$ F1); these metrics decouple (EM leads AUROC at 0.749 but ranks third in $\\rho$), so single-metric evaluation risks selecting the wrong signal.\n\n    \\item \\textbf{LP score compression and MBR utility-quality misalignment.}\n    Despite strong quality estimation ($\\rho$ up to 0.38, AUROC up to 0.75), no signal significantly improves selection despite 12--18~pp of oracle headroom: LP scores are compressed into effective ties (60\\% of instances), and MBR-style selection correlates with F1 across instances but not within them.\n    Where compression is mitigated, LP becomes significant ($+2.9$~pp, $p{=}0.01$), confirming score compression as the bottleneck.\n\n    \\item \\textbf{Task-complexity boundary conditions.}\n    Signal rankings shift with difficulty (EM leads on easy tasks, SJ on hard), and at the capability boundary (WNUT-17), conditional correlations collapse to non-significance while LP exhibits strong negative correlation ($\\rho{=}{-}0.410$).\n    NER findings span three datasets; RE conclusions rest on SciERC alone (preliminary).\n\\end{enumerate}\n\n",

_source line 4576 · 2026-05-15T15:05:39.831Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01KDpr7q4RAffcgmzEodQKj3`</summary>

```text
55	
56	\paragraph{Difficulty-dependent signal complementarity.}
57	On SciERC NER (6 types), structural signals lead: VC $\rho{=}0.379$, SJ $\rho{=}0.360$, both outperforming surface ($\rho{\leq}0.295$) and LP ($\rho{=}0.205$); this advantage persists on RE (VC $\rho{=}0.350$; preliminary).
58	The best AUROC reaches 0.749 (EM), indicating strong binary discriminative power; pairwise bootstrap CIs confirm the SJ--LP gap ($\Delta\rho{=}{+}0.155$, CI excludes zero) but the SJ--EM gap is marginal (Appendix~\ref{app:bootstrap_signal}).
59	
60	\paragraph{$\rho$--AUROC dissociation.}
61	Signal choice depends on the intended use: on NER, EM leads AUROC (0.749) despite ranking third in $\rho$ (0.295), preferred for binary quality triage; structural signals ($\rho{\geq}0.36$) are better for fine-grained ranking.
62	On CoNLL with LLaMA, LP leads AUROC (0.761) yet trails in $\rho$ (0.307), confirming this pattern across datasets.
63	
64	\paragraph{Signal rankings shift with task difficulty.}
65	On CoNLL (greedy F1${\approx}$0.91), EM leads ($\rho{=}0.451$, AUROC${=}0.816$); this crossover---EM${>}$SJ on easy tasks vs.\ SJ${>}$EM on hard tasks---demonstrates difficulty-dependent complementarity, consistent across model families ($\Delta{=}0.005$, within 3-seed $\sigma{\approx}0.007$).
66	On WNUT-17 ($N{=}16$, 52\% zero F1), full-set correlations are misleading: VC achieves $\rho{=}0.724$ but conditional $\rho{=}0.038$ (n.s.; Table~\ref{tab:wnut}), while LP exhibits strong \textit{negative} correlation ($\rho{=}{-}0.410$), likely reflecting overconfidence on OOD inputs \citep{xiong2024llm}.
67	On the conditional set, all signals collapse toward weak or non-significant correlations, suggesting a capability boundary beyond which consistency signals capture binary success/failure but not quality gradation.
68	
69	\paragraph{Conditional filtering.}
70	Restricting to non-zero F1 instances reshuffles signal rankings (Figure~\ref{fig:signal_heatmap}, right): EM emerges as the strongest conditional signal on both NER ($\rho{=}0.360$) and RE ($\rho{=}0.417$; preliminary), while structural signals drop substantially (VC NER: $0.379{\to}0.300$) and LP RE collapses to $\rho{=}0.011$ (Appendix~\ref{app:additional}).
71	
72	%% ============================================================
73	\subsection{The Correlation-Selection Gap}
74	\label{sec:selection_gap}
```
